## Claude · 2026-04-11T18:58:37.501Z

# The Problem: What Deaf People Face

---

## 1. Scale of the Problem

### Global Numbers

| Metric | Number |
|---|---|
| People with some hearing loss globally | **1.5 billion** (20% of world population) |
| People with disabling hearing loss requiring rehabilitation | **430 million** |
| Deaf children globally | **34 million** |
| Children aged 5-19 with hearing loss | **95.1 million** |
| Living in developing countries | **~344 million** (80% of total) |
| Projected by 2050 | **2.5 billion** with hearing loss, **700+ million** needing rehabilitation |
| Young adults at risk from unsafe listening | **1+ billion** |
| Childhood hearing loss that is preventable | **~60%** |

The greatest burden is in South Asia, Asia-Pacific, and Sub-Saharan Africa. Nearly 80% of people with disabling hearing loss live in low- and middle-income countries.

In the United States: 65.3% of Americans aged 71+ have hearing loss. Only ~2,038 certified sign language interpreters serve the entire country — a ratio of roughly **50 deaf people to every 1 interpreter**.

---

## 2. Economic Impact

### Global Cost

WHO estimates unaddressed hearing loss costs the global economy **~$980 billion per year**:

| Cost Category | Annual Amount |
|---|---|
| Quality-of-life losses | $457 billion (47%) |
| Additional healthcare costs | $314 billion (32%) |
| Lost productivity | $182 billion+ |

Every **$1 invested** in hearing care returns nearly **$16** over 10 years. It would cost less than $1.40 per person annually to scale services globally. A 90% reduction in unaddressed hearing loss would yield **$2.4 trillion** in productivity benefits.

### Individual Economic Impact (United States)

| Metric | Deaf/HoH | Hearing | Gap |
|---|---|---|---|
| Employment rate | 54% | 70% | 16 points |
| Mean annual wage | $23,481 | $31,272 | **25% less** |
| High school graduation | 6% lower | — | — |
| College enrollment (18-64) | 5% | 11% | Half the rate |

### Intersectional Disparities

| Group | In Labor Force |
|---|---|
| Deaf White Americans | 59% |
| Deaf African Americans | 44.8% |
| Deaf Native Americans | 43.6% |
| Deaf women | Earn 77 cents per dollar deaf men earn |
| Deaf with additional disabilities | 32% employed |
| Deafblind | 35% employed |

Education is the strongest lever: employment rises from 31.7% (no high school diploma) to 74.4% (master's degree).

---

## 3. Education Crisis

### Literacy

This is one of the most staggering statistics in all of education:

- Median reading level of deaf high school graduates has remained at **4th grade level for over a century**
- ~20% graduate reading at or below **2nd grade level**
- ~33% read between **2nd and 4th grade level**
- Only **7-10%** read at 7th grade level or above

That means the vast majority of deaf students leave school functionally unable to read at an adult level — and this has not improved in 100 years.

### Language Deprivation — The Root Cause

- **90%** of deaf children are born to hearing parents
- **88%** of those parents never learn sign language
- Only **8%** of deaf children receive regular home sign language access
- The critical period for language acquisition is approximately **ages 0-5**

Missing this window leads to **Language Deprivation Syndrome** — a neurodevelopmental condition with permanent consequences:

**Symptoms:**
- **Language dysfluency** — limited vocabulary restricted to concrete objects/actions, no time markers, disrupted communication
- **Knowledge gaps** — ignorance of how society functions (government, health systems, employment) because they were excluded from family conversations where children normally absorb this
- **Disruptions in thinking, mood, and behavior** — impulsivity, aggression, poor emotional regulation, difficulty problem-solving
- **Permanent neurological changes** — measurable brain differences on neuroimaging

**How severe is this?** In one study, **75% of deaf psychiatric inpatients** were classified as language-deprived. Only 28% actually had psychotic disorders — the rest were hospitalized because language deprivation symptoms were **misdiagnosed as mental illness**.

**The cruel irony:** Signing children with cochlear implants **outperform** non-signing children with implants in speech and language outcomes. Yet medical professionals still treat sign language as a "last resort."

### Developing World

- **75%** of deaf children worldwide have no access to formal education (UNESCO)
- Hearing aid production meets less than **10%** of global need
- Less than **3%** of developing countries' needs are met
- Ear specialists and audiologists barely exist in low-income countries

---

## 4. Mental Health Crisis

### The Numbers

- Deaf adults and youth report **higher rates of depression and anxiety** than hearing peers
- Suicide attempt rate among deaf individuals: **up to 5x higher** than the general population
- 81% of Deaf LGBTQ youth reported recent depression (vs 68% hearing LGBTQ youth)
- **55%** of Deaf LGBTQ youth seriously considered suicide in the past year (vs 39% hearing)
- **26%** attempted suicide in the past year (vs 14% hearing)

### Why This Happens

A 2025 study found that language deprivation creates "an interplay of frustration, isolation and disconnection with hearing people, as well as thoughts of suicide." The drivers:

1. **Communication barriers** accessing therapy — most therapists don't know ASL
2. **Cumulative social exclusion** — from family, school, workplace, everywhere
3. **Misdiagnosis** — language deprivation confused with psychotic disorders leads to wrong treatment
4. **Audism** — systemic discrimination against deaf people, often invisible to the hearing world

### Dinner Table Syndrome

This term, coined by the deaf community, captures the daily reality of isolation better than any statistic.

90% of deaf children are born to hearing parents. 88% of those parents never learn sign language. Every night at the dinner table, the family talks, laughs, argues, shares stories — and the deaf child sits there understanding **nothing**. They watch faces move. They see people react. They are completely shut out.

This happens at every meal. Every family gathering. Every holiday. For years.

"For many deaf and hard-of-hearing people, repeated exclusion at mealtimes can lead to feelings of isolation, frustration, and low self-esteem." Many deaf adults **dread** going home for holidays because of this accumulated exclusion.

The dinner table is supposed to be where a family connects. For a deaf child in a hearing family, it's where they learn they are invisible.

---

## 5. Healthcare Barriers

### Access to Care

- Healthcare is "routinely inaccessible" to deaf people
- Only ~10,000 certified ASL interpreters in the US and Canada **combined**
- In some areas: **3 interpreters** serving **800+ deaf people**
- **59%** of addiction treatment facilities provide no ASL support
- **41%** of mental health facilities provide no ASL support

### What Happens Without an Interpreter

When deaf patients don't have qualified interpreters:

- **Misdiagnosis** — symptoms and medical history communicated incorrectly
- **Medication errors** — instructions misunderstood, wrong dosage taken
- **No informed consent** — patients agree to procedures they don't understand
- **Longer treatment times** — delays from communication failures
- **Lower satisfaction** — systemic neglect in emergency care

Using family members to interpret is both an ADA violation and a source of errors. Yet it happens constantly.

### Emergency Departments

Deaf patients experience significantly longer treatment times and lower satisfaction. Interpreter services are technically available in many hospitals but are "inconsistently implemented and frequently bypassed altogether." In an emergency — stroke, heart attack, car accident — the deaf patient may have no way to communicate what's wrong.

---

## 6. Life-and-Death: Emergency Services & Police

### 911 Access

- Text-to-911 is available in **less than 10%** of the US
- Traditional 911 is voice-only — completely inaccessible to deaf people
- Deaf people report fear about accessing emergency services
- Advocates say this inaccessibility "has cost in terms of loss of life and physical harm"

### Police Encounters

This is where the communication gap becomes lethal. When officers shout commands and a deaf person doesn't comply — because they literally cannot hear — police have responded with fatal force:

- **Magdiel Sanchez** (Oklahoma City, 2017) — holding a metal pipe in his front yard, shot and killed by police. Neighbors were screaming "he can't hear you." Officers fired anyway.
- **Daniel Harris** — shot and killed for not complying with orders he couldn't perceive
- **Darnel Wicker** — same pattern, same outcome
- **John Williams** (Seattle) — Deaf Native American wood carver, shot by police while holding a carving knife and a block of wood
- **January 2026** — another deaf man shot and killed by police for not following verbal commands

The National Association of the Deaf: "Too many deaf and hard of hearing people have died at the hands of police who denied them effective communication."

**There is no national training policy** for police interactions with deaf people. Not even after multiple high-profile killings.

### Mass Shooting Vulnerability

Four deaf people died in the Lewiston, Maine mass shooting (2023). They couldn't hear gunshots, warnings, or evacuation instructions. CNN reported on "their unique vulnerabilities during a crisis" — a crisis that hearing people at least had the chance to flee from.

---

## 7. Workplace Discrimination and Exclusion

### The Daily Experience

A survey of British deaf workers found:

- **83%** excluded from conversations with colleagues
- **69%** experience loneliness at work
- **59%** excluded from social events
- **34%** experience bullying and unkindness

This isn't about accommodation requests being denied. This is about the fundamental experience of going to work every day and being invisible. Missing the jokes. Missing the context. Missing the relationships that drive careers.

### The Interpreter Shortage

The structural problem is inescapable:

- 50:1 deaf-to-interpreter ratio nationally
- 18% of college interpreters report burnout (2025)
- VRS interpreters face "unsafe working conditions" causing mass attrition
- Intensive certification requirements + high turnover + growing demand = permanent shortage

Even when interpreters are available, they must be booked days in advance. The impromptu meeting? The hallway conversation with the CEO? The emergency team huddle? The deaf employee misses all of it.

### ADA Enforcement

- 8,000+ web accessibility lawsuits in 2024 (7% increase from 2023)
- First violation: up to $75,000 fine. Subsequent: up to $150,000
- But enforcement is inconsistent — many businesses never provide required accommodations
- The law exists. Compliance doesn't.

---

## 8. Digital Divide

- **16 percentage point gap** in smartphone ownership: 72% of disabled Americans vs 88% of non-disabled
- Videos and podcasts without captions exclude deaf users entirely
- Social media platforms have been slow — TikTok took years to add auto-captioning; Clubhouse still has none
- Online medical consultations often inaccessible
- Remote work requires accommodations that many employers don't provide

The irony: technology is supposed to be the great equalizer. But when it's built without deaf people in mind, it creates another barrier.

---

## 9. Legal System & Public Services

### Courts

- Deaf individuals face "significant barriers when navigating the legal system, particularly in court proceedings where auditory communication is the norm"
- Without interpreters, deaf defendants risk "missing critical information, misunderstanding legal jargon, or being unable to fully participate in their own defense"
- A deaf person can be convicted of a crime they didn't fully understand they were charged with

### Prisons

- Deaf people "tend to be imprisoned more often, incarcerated longer, and returned to prison more quickly than others"
- Incarcerated deaf people may never learn prison rules
- Can't defend themselves in disciplinary hearings
- Can't communicate with guards, medical staff, counselors, or family
- Can't hear meal call

### Voting

Despite ADA protections, deaf and disabled voters face "inaccessible polling sites, inaccessible online voter registration, and inaccessible voting machines."

---

## 10. Media Representation

- **56%** of deaf consumers say they "rarely" or "never" see themselves in film/TV
- **63%** say portrayals use negative images
- **70%** say deaf characters are shown as "objects of pity or in need of help"
- **76%** believe these portrayals shape how they're treated in real life
- Only **6%** have ever seen a deaf LGBTQ+ person in fiction
- Hearing audiences are **2x more likely** to have seen white deaf characters vs deaf people of color
- **82%** say the industry needs more career support for deaf professionals
- Deaf people are underrepresented in writing, directing, and producing

The stories told about deaf people are overwhelmingly told by hearing people — and they get it wrong in ways that reinforce the barriers.

---

## 11. The Daily Reality

Beyond statistics, here's what a deaf person navigates **every single day**:

**Morning:** Alarm must be visual or vibrating. Can't hear smoke alarm without specialized equipment. Can't call to schedule appointments — must use relay services that many offices don't understand or accept.

**Commute:** Can't hear train announcements, construction warnings, car horns. Emergency vehicle sirens are inaudible. Missed stops, missed warnings, missed dangers.

**Work:** Excluded from hallway conversations, impromptu meetings, watercooler talk. Must request interpreters days in advance. Conference calls inaccessible without CART or an interpreter. Miss jokes, context, the social bonding that drives promotions.

**Errands:** Ordering coffee requires pointing or writing. Drive-throughs are impossible. Bank teller conversations through glass are inaccessible. Doctor visits without an interpreter mean guessing at medical instructions for your own body.

**Home:** Dinner Table Syndrome. Doorbell must be visual. Can't hear a baby crying without monitoring technology. Can't hear an intruder.

**Emergency:** Can't call 911 in 90%+ of the country. If police approach, not hearing commands could be fatal. In a fire, can't hear the alarm without someone physically waking you.

**Entertainment:** Movies without captions. Live shows without interpreters. Music largely inaccessible. Video games with audio-only cues.

As one source puts it: "For most people, everyday tasks such as going to the supermarket, ordering a drink at a coffee shop or phoning the GP are so routine that they barely require thought. For members of the Deaf community, these same activities can be filled with invisible barriers or moments of exclusion."

---

## 12. The Core Problem Statement

The fundamental issue isn't that deaf people can't hear.

**The fundamental issue is that the entire world is built around hearing.**

Every system — education, healthcare, employment, emergency services, entertainment, family life, legal proceedings, public services — assumes auditory communication as the default. When you can't access the default, you're locked out of everything.

The interpreter shortage (50:1 ratio) means deaf people cannot access the basic infrastructure of society on their own terms. The education system fails them — 4th grade reading level at high school graduation, unchanged for a century. The healthcare system endangers them — misdiagnosis, no interpreters in emergencies. The legal system imprisons them longer. Police kill them for not hearing commands. Their own families exclude them from conversation 88% of the time.

**The cost:**
- $980 billion/year globally
- $182 billion in lost productivity alone
- Suicide rates 5x higher
- Employment 16 points lower
- Income 25% less
- Reading at 4th grade level after 12 years of school

**The opportunity:**
- Every $1 invested returns $16
- 60% of childhood hearing loss is preventable
- Language Deprivation Syndrome is entirely preventable with early sign language access
- Technology that bridges the communication gap — not replacing interpreters, but filling the 90% of moments where no interpreter is available — could transform outcomes for 430 million people

---

Sources:
- [WHO: Deafness and Hearing Loss](https://www.who.int/news-room/fact-sheets/detail/deafness-and-hearing-loss)
- [Lancet: Global Hearing Loss Burden](https://www.thelancet.com/journals/langlo/article/PIIS2214-109X(21)00447-2/fulltext)
- [National Deaf Center: Employment Report](https://nationaldeafcenter.org/news-items/employment-report-shows-strong-labor-market-passing-deaf-americans/)

## Claude · 2026-04-11T19:12:48.769Z

From the ASL research:

| Dataset | Language | Type | Size | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **WLASL** | ASL | Isolated signs | 2,000 classes, ~21K videos | Most-used ASL isolated benchmark |
| **MS-ASL** | ASL | Isolated signs | 1,000 classes, 25K+ videos | 222 signers, Microsoft |
| **ASL Citizen** | ASL | Isolated signs | 83,000 videos, 2,700+ signs | Crowd-sourced from Deaf community |
| **How2Sign** | ASL | Continuous | 80 hours, 35K sentences | Largest clean ASL continuous dataset |
| **YouTube-ASL** | ASL | Continuous | ~1,000 hours | Noisy, scraped from YouTube, Google 2024 |
| **OpenASL** | ASL | Continuous | 288 hours | Large-scale, from online ASL content |
| **PHOENIX-2014T** | German SL | Continuous | 8,257 sentences | Weather broadcasts, most benchmarked CSLR dataset |
| **CSL-Daily** | Chinese SL | Continuous | 20,654 sentences | Daily conversation domain |
| **ASLLRP** | ASL | Mixed | 2,119 English-gloss pairs | Used by Apple's CHI 2025 research for text→gloss training |
| **ASL Alphabet** | ASL | Fingerspelling | — | Static hand shapes, up to 99.97% accuracy |
| **AfriSpeech-200** | Nigerian English | Speech (not sign) | — | Relevant to your Sabi work, not ASL |

The key takeaway was the **1,000x data gap** — speech recognition trains on 680,000+ hours (Whisper), while the largest ASL dataset is ~1,000 hours of noisy YouTube scrapes. That's the single biggest bottleneck holding back sign language AI.

## Claude · 2026-04-11T19:20:03.171Z

Here's what's actually available to download and use:

## Pretrained Models You Can Get Right Now

### Sign Language Recognition (ASL→English)

| Model | What it does | Where to get it | Open source? |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Google SignGemma** | Full ASL→English translation, on-device, <200ms | Developer preview at [developers.google.com/signgemma](https://discuss.ai.google.dev/t/signgemma-availability/111432) — request API key | Limited preview (not fully open) |
| **SignVLM** | CLIP-based video model for sign recognition. Outperforms on WLASL, KArSL, LSA64. Zero-shot and few-shot capable. | [github.com/Hamzah-Luqman/signVLM](https://github.com/Hamzah-Luqman/signVLM) | Yes — code + weights |
| **CorrNet+** | Continuous SLR AND translation from RGB video. Demo supports multi-image and video input. | [github.com/hulianyuyy/CorrNet](https://github.com/hulianyuyy/CorrNet) | Yes — code + weights |
| **SLRT** (EMNLP 2024) | Online continuous sign language recognition + translation | [github.com/FangyunWei/SLRT](https://github.com/FangyunWei/SLRT) | Yes — code + models |
| **MediaPipe Holistic** | Real-time hand/face/body landmark extraction (543 keypoints). Not sign recognition itself, but the foundation most systems build on. | [Google MediaPipe](https://ai.google.dev/edge/mediapipe) | Yes — fully open |
| **ASL YOLO models** | Letter-level ASL detection (A-Y) | [huggingface.co/atalaydenknalbant/asl-yolo-models](https://huggingface.co/atalaydenknalbant/asl-yolo-models) | Yes |

### Sign Language Generation (English→ASL)

| Model | What it does | Where to get it | Open source? |
|---|---|---|---|
| **SignLLM** (ICCV 2025) | Sign language PRODUCTION using LLMs — English→sign poses | [Paper (ICCV 2025)](https://openaccess.thecvf.com/content/ICCV2025W/CV4A11y/papers/Fang_SignLLM_Sign_Language_Production_Large_Language_Models_ICCVW_2025_paper.pdf) | Paper available, check for code release |
| **Uni-Sign** | Unified model — both recognition AND translation, large-scale pretraining | [OpenReview paper](https://openreview.net/pdf/b769e431ddd7c8f48751bd22d3df5c4710df3624.pdf) | Check for code release |

### Datasets on HuggingFace

| Dataset | Link | What's in it |
|---|---|---|
| **WLASL** | [Voxel51/WLASL](https://huggingface.co/datasets/Voxel51/WLASL) | 2,000 ASL words, ~21K videos |
| **ASL-MNIST** | [Voxel51/American-Sign-Language-MNIST](https://huggingface.co/datasets/Voxel51/American-Sign-Language-MNIST) | 34,627 samples, alphabet |
| **NCSLGR** | [ncslgr/ncslgr](https://huggingface.co/datasets/ncslgr/ncslgr) | Native signer ASL video with non-manual feature annotations |

### The Big Picture

- **Best ready-to-use for recognition:** SignGemma (if you get developer access) or CorrNet+ (fully open)
- **Best foundation to build on:** MediaPipe for keypoint extraction → your own transformer on top
- **Best for research:** SignVLM (zero-shot capable, good across multiple sign languages)
- **Generation is behind:** SignLLM and Uni-Sign are cutting edge but may not have weights released yet
- **Nothing end-to-end bidirectional** is available as a downloadable model

Want me to dig into any of these specifically?

## Naomi · 2026-04-11T19:32:50.145Z

what's another way for deaf people to parse language other than reading?

## Claude · 2026-04-11T19:33:10.182Z

In the context of receiving information (not producing it):

1. **Sign language** — their native/preferred language. Visual, spatial, fully grammatical. But requires the other person to know it.

2. **Visual sign language avatars** — what Signapse, VSL Labs, Sign-Speak are building. English speech → AI-generated signing displayed on a screen (or eventually glasses). This is the ideal for many deaf people because it's information delivered IN their language.

3. **Lip reading / speechreading** — only ~30-40% of English sounds are visible on the lips. Even the best lip readers catch maybe 30% of content and fill in the rest from context. It's exhausting and unreliable. Not a real solution.

4. **Cued Speech** — a visual system where hand shapes near the face disambiguate lip patterns. Makes spoken language fully visible. Small but dedicated community uses it.

5. **Haptic / tactile** — vibration patterns conveying information. Used especially by deafblind community (tactile sign language, Pro-Tactile ASL). Some research into vibrotactile devices that encode speech into skin patterns.

6. **Visual/gestural communication** — home signs, gestures, pointing, drawing, mime. How most deaf people communicate with hearing strangers who don't sign.

Are you thinking about this for the glasses product direction — specifically what to show the deaf user instead of (or in addition to) text captions?

## Claude · 2026-04-11T20:04:52.459Z

This is a strong concept. Let me map it directly against the technical architecture I laid out.

## How Your Idea Maps to the Optimal Architecture

What you're describing:

```
DEAF PERSON signs
    → HEARING PERSON's glasses cameras capture the signing
    → AI translates ASL → English
    → Audio plays through hearing person's glasses speaker
    → Hearing person HEARS the deaf person "speaking"

HEARING PERSON speaks
    → DEAF PERSON's glasses microphone captures speech
    → AI converts speech → text
    → Text displays on deaf person's glasses lens
    → Deaf person READS (or sees avatar signing) what was said
```

This is essentially the optimal system I described — but with one key insight that makes it **better than anything currently proposed by any company or research team.**

## What Makes This Smart

### The camera placement problem is solved

The single biggest hardware challenge I flagged was: **glasses cameras face outward — they can't see the wearer's own hands.** Every other approach (SignGlass, OmniBridge, etc.) struggles with this. The deaf person's glasses can't see the deaf person signing because the cameras point away from them.

Your design flips this: **the hearing person's glasses are the ones doing the sign recognition.** Their cameras face the deaf person directly — getting a perfect front view of:
- Both hands (full signing space, waist to above head)
- Face (non-manual markers — eyebrows, mouth morphemes, head tilt)
- Body (role shift, spatial referencing)

This is the ideal camera angle. SignGlass needed 3 cameras in awkward positions to capture both hands and face. Your design gets all of it from a single natural vantage point — the conversation partner's perspective.

### Both directions use the right modality

- Hearing person receives **audio** — natural for them
- Deaf person receives **visual** (text or avatar) — natural for them

Nobody's wearing something that fights their natural communication mode.

### The easy direction is already solved

**Hearing→Deaf** (speech to captions on glasses) is shipping technology today. XRAI AR2, Hearview, TranscribeGlass — they all do exactly this. The deaf person's glasses are essentially a caption glasses product that already exists and works at 95% accuracy.

**You only have to solve one hard problem** — the ASL→English direction on the hearing person's glasses.

### It's socially natural

Both people wear similar glasses. They maintain eye contact. No phone screen between them. No kiosk. No laptop. Just two people having a conversation. The technology disappears.

## How It Maps to the Best Technical Approaches

### The hearing person's glasses (ASL→English)

```
Outward cameras capture deaf person signing
    ↓
On-device MediaPipe: extract 543 keypoints per frame (~5ms)
    ↓
Keypoints compressed → sent to cloud (~50-100kbps)
    ↓
Cloud: SignGemma-class transformer model → English text
    ↓
On-device TTS → audio through glasses speaker or bone conduction
```

This uses:
- **SignGlass's insight** (capture both manual and non-manual markers) — but from a better camera angle
- **Google SignGemma's approach** (vision transformer + language model)
- **MediaPipe's efficiency** (lightweight keypoint extraction on-device, heavy inference in cloud)

Estimated latency: **150-500ms** — slightly delayed but conversational.

### The deaf person's glasses (English→visual)

```
Microphone captures hearing person's speech
    ↓
On-device Whisper-class STT (~50-100ms)
    ↓
Text displayed as captions on AR lens
```

This is just... XRAI AR2. It exists. The hard engineering is done.

**Future upgrade path:** Instead of text, display a VSL Labs / Signapse-style signing avatar on the lens. This would let the deaf person receive information in ASL rather than English text — massively better for people whose primary language is ASL (remember, median deaf reading level is 4th grade). But captions work as the v1.

## Honest Assessment: Strengths and Weaknesses

### Strengths

| Strength | Why it matters |
|---|---|
| Camera angle is optimal | Best possible view of signer's hands + face — better than any existing prototype |
| Hearing→deaf direction is solved tech | Caption glasses already ship at $95-$880 |
| Socially natural | Eye contact maintained, technology invisible |
| Symmetrical design | Both people wear similar devices — no stigma, no asymmetry |
| Modular upgrade path | Start with captions for deaf user, upgrade to avatar later |
| Addresses the "return path" problem | The #1 criticism of all previous tech (gloves, single-direction systems) is that they only solve one direction. This solves both. |

### Challenges

| Challenge | Severity | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| **ASL→English accuracy** | High | Best current: 83% BLEU-1 (SignGlass), ~40-60% WER on natural conversation. Not good enough for reliable communication yet. |
| **Both people need glasses** | Medium | Adoption barrier — hearing person has to buy and wear glasses too. Why would they? They need a reason. |
| **Non-manual markers** | High | The hearing person's glasses cameras CAN see the deaf person's face — but the AI still can't reliably interpret all grammatical facial markers (~80% detection). |
| **Classifier predicates** | Very high | Still completely unsolved by any system. No model handles productive/novel signs. |
| **Cost** | Medium | Two pairs of smart AR glasses = $1,500-2,000+ at current prices |
| **Battery life** | Medium | The hearing person's glasses run the heavy vision pipeline — 2-3 hours max. The deaf person's glasses just do speech-to-text — could last 6-8 hours. |
| **Latency** | Medium | 150-500ms delay means the hearing person hears the translation slightly after the deaf person finishes a phrase. Conversational but not instantaneous. |
| **Cloud dependency** | Medium | The heavy ASL recognition model likely needs cloud compute. No internet = no translation. |

### The "Why Would the Hearing Person Wear Them?" Problem

This is the biggest adoption question. The deaf person's motivation is obvious — communication access. But the hearing person needs a reason to put on glasses. Possible answers:

- **Workplace/institutional use** — employer provides them for meetings with deaf employees/customers
- **Medical/legal settings** — hospitals and courts provide them when no interpreter is available
- **Family** — hearing family members wear them at home (addresses Dinner Table Syndrome directly)
- **Multi-purpose glasses** — the hearing person's glasses also do language translation, navigation, notifications, etc. Sign recognition is one feature among many
- **The glasses are cheap enough to be casual** — like handing someone a pair of TranscribeGlass ($95)

## How Good Is This Solution?

**It's the best architectural concept I've seen.** Here's why:

1. **It's better than SignGlass** — same concept but better camera angle (conversation partner's view vs self-mounted cameras with jitter)
2. **It's better than OmniBridge** — portable glasses vs an AI PC
3. **It's better than Sign-Speak** — wearable vs screen/QR code
4. **It's truly bidirectional** — not half-solutions
5. **It solves the return-path problem** that the Deaf community has criticized for decades
6. **The easy half is already solved** — you're not building two impossible things, just one hard thing + one solved thing

**Where it sits on the timeline:**

- **v1 (buildable in 1-2 years):** Hearing person's glasses capture video → cloud recognition of common signs/fingerspelling → audio. Deaf person's glasses do speech-to-text captions. Limited vocabulary but functional for basic conversation.
- **v2 (3-5 years):** Continuous sign recognition with NMMs, larger vocabulary, lower latency. Avatar option on deaf person's lens.
- **v3 (5-10 years):** Full conversational ASL with classifier predicates, spatial grammar, on-device processing, all-day battery.

The v1 alone — even with limited vocabulary — would be **the first product in the world that lets a deaf person and a hearing person have a face-to-face conversation through wearable technology with no interpreter.** That's a fundable, demonstrable, competition-winning idea.

## Claude · 2026-04-11T20:23:35.317Z

I've read every file. Here's the evaluation.

---

## Tomisin777/asl-translator — Full Evaluation

### What It Is

A Python/Flask bidirectional ASL translation prototype using two webcams, MediaPipe hand tracking, Claude Haiku for sign interpretation, Google Speech Recognition for the hearing person, and pyttsx3 for text-to-speech. It has a dashboard UI and a "glasses display" page.

### Architecture (Matches Your Concept)

```
DEAF PERSON signs
  → Camera 0 captures hands
  → MediaPipe extracts 21 landmarks per hand
  → Landmarks described as text (fingers extended, wrist position)
  → Claude Haiku interprets: "What ASL sign is this?"
  → Result spoken aloud via pyttsx3

HEARING PERSON speaks
  → Microphone captures speech
  → Google Speech Recognition → raw text
  → Claude Haiku cleans the text
  → Displayed on glasses.html (full-screen text on phone)
```

This IS your two-glasses concept implemented as a hackathon demo. The "glasses" are phones opening `http://IP:5000/glasses`.

---

### What's Good

| Strength | Detail |
|---|---|
| **The concept is right** | Bidirectional — both directions implemented. Matches the optimal architecture from our research. |
| **It works as a demo** | Two cameras, real-time WebSocket updates, clean dashboard UI, phone-as-glasses display |
| **Clean code structure** | Modular: `asl_detector.py`, `claude_api.py`, `speech.py`, `app.py` — each file has one job |
| **Dashboard is polished** | Dark theme, live badges, dual camera feeds, projector-style split view, start/stop controls. Looks professional for a hackathon. |
| **Glasses page is smart** | Full-screen black + white text, 14vw font size, keeps screen awake, flash animation on update. Simple and effective. |
| **Test suite** | `test.py` lets you test camera, speech, Claude, and full pipeline independently. Good for debugging. |
| **Thread safety** | Locks on shared frames, daemon threads for cameras and detection loops. Won't crash from race conditions. |

---

### What's Weak (Against Our Research)

This is where the research context matters. Here's what the current implementation is doing vs what the state of the art requires:

#### 1. ASL Recognition — Fundamentally Limited

**Current approach:** MediaPipe extracts hand landmarks → text description like "fingers extended = index, middle | wrist at x=0.50, y=0.60" → Claude Haiku guesses the sign.

**The problem:** This is essentially asking an LLM to guess an ASL sign from a text description of finger positions. Claude has no actual ASL training data. It's pattern-matching from its general knowledge of "what hand shapes correspond to what signs." This is:

- **Not real sign language recognition** — it's an LLM guessing
- **No temporal modeling** — ASL signs are defined by MOVEMENT, not static hand positions. A single frame can't distinguish signs that differ only in motion (e.g., "chair" vs "sit" — same handshape, different movement)
- **No non-manual markers** — no facial expression capture at all. Misses questions, negation, conditionals, all grammatical markers
- **No continuous recognition** — processes one frame at a time with a 1.5s interval. Real signing is fluid and continuous
- **Limited to ~26 static handshapes** — basically fingerspelling-level at best

**What it should be:** MediaPipe keypoints → temporal sequence (multiple frames) → trained sign language recognition model (CorrNet+, SignVLM, or fine-tuned transformer). Not an LLM guessing from a text description.

#### 2. Confidence is Hardcoded

```python
socketio.emit('asl_detected', {
    'sign': sentence,
    'confidence': '94%'  # ← This is fake
})
```

The confidence is always "94%". There's no actual confidence metric from the recognition pipeline.

#### 3. Landmark Description is Too Sparse

```python
desc = f"Hand {i+1}: fingers extended = {', '.join(extended) if extended else 'none (fist)'}"
desc += f" | wrist at x={hand[0]['x']:.2f}, y={hand[0]['y']:.2f}"
```

This only sends:
- Which fingers are extended (binary up/down)
- Wrist position

It throws away: finger curl degree, thumb opposition, palm orientation, hand rotation, distance between hands, movement direction, speed — all of which are phonemic in ASL.

#### 4. Speech→Deaf Direction Works But Is Basic

The hearing→deaf direction (speech recognition → text on screen) works and is the easier solved problem. Using Google Speech Recognition is fine for a demo. But:
- No noise cancellation optimized for the scenario
- The Claude "clean speech" step adds latency and isn't necessary
- No multi-language support

#### 5. No Actual Glasses Hardware

The "glasses" are phones opening a webpage. For a hackathon demo this is smart and practical. But the path to actual glasses hardware isn't addressed.

#### 6. Bad Word Filter Is Brittle

```python
BAD_WORDS = {
    'UNKNOWN', 'Error', 'error', 'multiple', 'signs',
    'information', 'identify', 'accurately', 'represent',
    'unknown', 'unclear', 'unsure'
}
```

This is filtering out words Claude says when it doesn't know the sign — which means Claude is frequently failing and the system is hiding it.

#### 7. Lip Reading Function Exists But Isn't Used

`claude_api.py` has `lip_read_description()` but it's never called from `app.py`. Dead code.

---

### Overall Assessment

| Dimension | Rating | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| **Concept** | Excellent | The two-camera bidirectional architecture is exactly right |
| **Hackathon demo quality** | Good | Clean UI, works end-to-end, testable |
| **ASL recognition accuracy** | Poor | LLM guessing from landmark descriptions ≠ real sign recognition |
| **Technical depth** | Shallow | No trained models, no temporal analysis, no NMMs |
| **Scalability** | Low | Can't scale beyond single-word guessing without real ML models |
| **Code quality** | Good | Clean, modular, threaded properly |
| **Path to product** | Needs rethinking | The LLM-as-recognizer approach is a dead end for real ASL |

### The Honest Summary

**As a hackathon demo: solid B+.** It demonstrates the concept, has a polished UI, works bidirectionally, and the phone-as-glasses idea is creative. Judges can see the vision.

**As actual ASL translation: it doesn't work.** Sending "fingers extended = index, middle" to Claude Haiku and asking it to guess the sign is not sign language recognition. It's a parlor trick. ASL signs are defined by handshape + location + movement + orientation + non-manual markers over time. A single frame's finger positions aren't enough to distinguish most signs.

### What Would Make It Real

The gap between "hackathon demo" and "functional prototype" is:

1. **Replace Claude-as-recognizer** with an actual sign recognition model (CorrNet+, SignVLM, or a custom transformer trained on WLASL/How2Sign). Keep Claude for the speech-cleaning if you want, but signs need a real model.

2. **Capture temporal sequences** — buffer 30-60 frames of keypoints and feed the sequence to the model, not individual frames.

3. **Add facial landmark extraction** — MediaPipe Face Mesh (468 landmarks) alongside the hand tracking. Feed both to the model.

4. **Real confidence scores** from the model's softmax output, not hardcoded "94%".

5. **Richer landmark description** if you keep the LLM approach short-term — include finger curl angles, palm direction, hand distance from body, movement trajectory over last N frames.

The concept is right. The architecture is right. The recognition engine needs to be replaced with real ML.

## Claude · 2026-04-11T20:38:08.447Z

For your current phase (Phase 0-1, the hackathon/demo stage), let me map what you have to what you need:

### What You Have → What It Does

| Hardware | Role |
|---|---|
| **Webcam (camera 0)** | Hearing person's "glasses" — captures deaf person signing |
| **Computer mic** | Deaf person's "glasses" — captures hearing person's speech |
| **Computer speakers / Echo Dot** | Speaks the translated signs aloud for the hearing person |
| **Computer screen / Projector** | Shows captions for the deaf person (the "glasses display") |
| **Laptop(s)** | Runs the Flask app, MediaPipe, model inference |

### Do You Need Anything Else?

**For a hackathon demo: No.** You have everything. The setup would be:

```
DEAF PERSON sits facing webcam
  → Webcam captures their signing
  → Laptop processes it
  → Echo Dot / speakers say the English translation aloud

HEARING PERSON speaks normally
  → Laptop mic picks up speech
  → Projector / second screen shows captions for deaf person to read
```

The projector is actually a great touch for a demo — judges can see the captions in real-time on a big screen. The Echo Dot gives clean audio output.

### One Suggestion

**A second webcam** ($15-20) would let you run both camera feeds simultaneously as the code currently expects (`cam_signing = cv2.VideoCapture(0)` and `cam_speaking = cv2.VideoCapture(1)`). Right now camera 1 points at the hearing person for the dashboard view. If you only have one webcam, you can simplify the code to use just camera 0 for sign recognition and skip the speaking-person camera feed — it's cosmetic, not functional.

**A second monitor or phone** is useful so the deaf person has their own screen showing captions (the `/glasses` endpoint), while the laptop shows the dashboard. But a phone on `http://YOUR_IP:5000/glasses` already handles this.

### Bottom Line

You're good. Webcam + Echo Dot + laptop + projector = a complete bidirectional demo. The only real investment needed is the **model** (software, free), not hardware.

## Claude · 2026-04-11T20:46:21.072Z

Here are my best suggestions, organized by angle:

### Named After the Concept (Two-Way Bridge)

| Name | Why |
|---|---|
| **BridgeSign** | Bridges the gap between signing and hearing. Simple, clear. |
| **Tether** | Two people tethered together in conversation. Elegant. |
| **Conduit** | The channel through which communication flows both ways. |
| **Relay** | Like a relay — passes the message both directions. Already familiar in deaf community (VRS = Video Relay Service). |

### Named After the Feeling (Connection)

| Name | Why |
|---|---|
| **Clarity** | What deaf people don't have in 90% of conversations. What this gives them. |
| **Unspoken** | The irony — translating what's "unspoken" (signed) into spoken words, and vice versa. Memorable, slightly poetic. |
| **Fluent** | The goal — making conversation between deaf and hearing feel fluent. Short, strong. |
| **Converse** | Literally what it enables. Also sounds like a brand (like the shoe company — already proven memorable). |

### Named After What It Replaces

| Name | Why |
|---|---|
| **50to1** | The interpreter shortage ratio. Provocative, memorable, tells the story of why this exists. |
| **NoWall** | The invisible wall between deaf and hearing people — this removes it. |

### Named in ASL/Deaf Culture Spirit

| Name | Why |
|---|---|
| **Dactyl** | From "dactylology" — the study of finger communication. Academic but cool-sounding. |
| **Palm** | Hands are the instrument. Simple, warm, human. |
| **SignLine** | Like a lifeline, but for sign. Clear what it does. |

### My Top 3

1. **Unspoken** — memorable, emotional, slightly provocative, works as a brand name, domain probably available
2. **Clarity** — clean, aspirational, describes the outcome not the tech
3. **BridgeSign** — most literal and immediately clear what it does

But honestly — the name should come from or be validated by the Deaf community. What resonates with hearing people might not resonate with the people this is actually for.

## Claude · 2026-04-11T21:22:38.535Z

Honest answer:

### DTW Matching Accuracy (what we're building)

**For the 30-50 signs in your recorded reference database:** ~75-85% accuracy. DTW matching against your own recorded references is reliable when:
- The signer is the same person who recorded the references (or similar signing style)
- The signs are distinct from each other (HELLO vs THANK-YOU are very different hand shapes/motions)
- Lighting and distance are consistent with recording conditions
- You record 3-5 examples per sign (handles variation)

**For fingerspelling:** ~85-90% on distinct letters (B, D, L, V, W, Y are easy). Drops to ~70% on similar letters (A/S/E/T look nearly identical from a camera).

### Where It Breaks Down

| Scenario | Accuracy | Why |
|---|---|---|
| Signs you recorded, same signer | ~80-85% | Best case — matching against yourself |
| Signs you recorded, different signer | ~60-70% | Everyone signs slightly differently |
| Signs NOT in your database | 0% | Returns "UNKNOWN" (honest, not guessing) |
| Fast/fluid continuous signing | ~50-60% | DTW struggles with coarticulation |
| Similar signs (MOTHER vs FATHER) | ~40-50% | Only differ in forehead vs chin contact — hard for DTW |
| Non-manual markers (questions vs statements) | Not yet | Face landmarks captured but not yet used in matching |

### Compared to the Current System

The current Claude-guessing approach is maybe ~15-25% accurate on real ASL signs. It's essentially random with some lucky guesses on obvious hand shapes (thumbs up, peace sign). **DTW at 75-85% on known signs is a massive improvement** — but it's still not conversational-quality.

### What Judges Will See

For a hackathon demo, you control the conditions:
- **You** pick which signs to demo (use the ones that work best)
- **You** record the references (match the demo signer)
- **You** control lighting, distance, background
- Fingerspelling as reliable backup for anything the DTW misses

Under controlled demo conditions: **~90%+ accuracy is achievable** because you're only demoing signs you know work.

### For Context: State of the Art

| System | Accuracy | Conditions |
|---|---|---|
| **Your current repo** (Claude guessing) | ~15-25% | Random on real ASL |
| **Dactyl after this build** (DTW, 50 signs) | ~75-85% | Known vocabulary, same signer |
| **Dactyl in demo conditions** | ~90%+ | Controlled, cherry-picked signs |
| **SignGlass** (UIST 2025, research SOTA) | 83% BLEU-1 | Lab conditions, 14 deaf participants |
| **Google SignGemma** | Unknown | Not independently benchmarked |
| **Human ASL interpreter** | ~99% | The gold standard |

You won't have conversational accuracy. But you'll have **the first working bidirectional demo with real pattern recognition** — and that's what wins a hackathon.

## Claude · 2026-04-11T21:28:23.415Z

Here's what the research shows — there are significantly better options than basic DTW.

---

## Better Than Basic DTW: Full Comparison

### The Core Problem with Basic DTW
Basic DTW compares raw x,y,z coordinates. These are NOT invariant — if the signer moves 6 inches to the left, or the camera angle changes, or they're further away, the coordinates change even though the sign is identical. This kills accuracy.

### Option 1: Angle-Based DTW (Best Bang for Buck)

Instead of matching raw coordinates, compute **20 inter-joint angles** from MediaPipe landmarks. Angles are mathematically invariant to position, rotation, and scale — if you sign HELLO close to the camera or far away, the angles between your finger joints are identical.

| Metric | Raw Coordinate DTW | Angle-Based DTW |
|---|---|---|
| Invariant to position | No | **Yes** |
| Invariant to rotation | No | **Yes** |
| Invariant to scale/distance | No | **Yes** |
| Accuracy improvement | Baseline | **+12 to +25 percentage points** |
| Training required | None | None |
| Setup complexity | Same as basic DTW | Same as basic DTW |

A 2026 paper showed angle features gave **+25.3 percentage points** over normalized raw coordinates on Arabic Sign Language and **+12.9 points** on LIBRAS — just by changing the feature representation. Same DTW algorithm, massively better features.

Another study using angle-based DTW achieved **66% top-5 and 75% top-10 on a 1,113 sign lexicon** — that's over a thousand signs with no training at all.

**Effort to implement:** ~1 hour more than basic DTW. Just compute angles instead of using raw coordinates.

Sources: [Geometry-Aware Metric Learning (2026)](https://arxiv.org/html/2603.09213), [Angle Based DTW for Sign Retrieval](https://link.springer.com/chapter/10.1007/978-3-032-14495-9_30)

---

### Option 2: Prototypical Networks (Most Impressive, Best Long-Term)

Instead of DTW distance matching, train a neural network that learns a **metric space** where similar signs cluster together. Then classify new signs by finding the nearest "prototype" (average embedding of a few examples).

**How it works:**
1. ST-GCN (Spatial-Temporal Graph Convolutional Network) encodes keypoint sequences
2. Multi-Scale Temporal Aggregation captures movements at different speeds (kernel sizes 3, 5, 7)
3. Attention pooling compresses variable-length sequences into fixed-size vectors
4. Classification = find nearest class prototype by Euclidean distance

**Key stat: Only needs 3 examples per sign to learn a new sign.** You show the system 3 examples of HELLO, and it can now recognize HELLO from anyone. This is the "wow" moment for judges: "We can add any new sign by showing it 3 times."

| Metric | Result |
|---|---|
| Top-1 accuracy (WLASL, 200 classes) | **43.75%** |
| Top-5 accuracy | **77.10%** |
| Top-10 accuracy | **84.50%** |
| vs baseline classifier | **+13 percentage points** |
| Examples needed per sign | **3** |
| Zero-shot on unseen dataset (SignASL) | 29.87% top-1, 44% top-5 |

**The killer feature:** Most confusions were linguistic synonyms (jail/prison, phone/telephone) — the model actually understands semantic similarity, not just hand shapes.

**Downside for hackathon:** Requires training the ST-GCN backbone on WLASL data. Even with pretrained weights, this is 2-4 hours of setup + training. Risky for 24 hours but doable.

Sources: [Few-Shot Prototypical Networks for ASL (2025)](https://arxiv.org/html/2512.10562)

---

### Option 3: MediaPipe + LSTM (Proven, Fast to Train)

Extract MediaPipe keypoints → feed temporal sequences to a small LSTM.

| System | Accuracy | Dataset |
|---|---|---|
| MP-GestLSTM | **94.38%** | 20 ASL word classes |
| MediaPipe + LSTM (Norwegian SL) | **95%** | Norwegian Sign Language |
| MediaPipe + BiLSTM (with angle features) | **96.3%** | Custom dataset |
| Random Forest on MediaPipe features | **98.83%** | Static signs |

**Downside:** These high accuracies are on small, controlled vocabularies (20-50 signs). On WLASL-scale (2,000 signs), LSTM accuracy drops significantly. Also requires training time.

**For a hackathon:** Could train a small LSTM on 30-50 signs using angle features from MediaPipe in ~30-60 minutes on CPU. Accuracy would be ~90-95% on your specific vocabulary. More reliable than DTW but requires the training step.

Sources: [MP-GestLSTM (2025)](https://www.tandfonline.com/doi/full/10.1080/21642583.2025.2587853), [SLRNet (2025)](https://arxiv.org/html/2506.11154v1)

---

### Option 4: Soft DTW (Differentiable, Trainable)

Standard DTW is not differentiable — you can't backpropagate through it. **Soft DTW** is a smooth approximation that CAN be used as a loss function in neural networks. This means you can train a model where DTW matching is the actual learning objective.

- CUDA implementation runs **100x faster** than CPU DTW
- Can train an encoder + Soft DTW end-to-end
- Gets the temporal flexibility of DTW + the learning power of neural networks

**Downside for hackathon:** Requires PyTorch + CUDA GPU for the fast version. More complex to implement.

Source: [pytorch-softdtw-cuda](https://github.com/Maghoumi/pytorch-softdtw-cuda)

---

### Option 5: DTW + Voting (Recent 2025 Approach)

A 2025 paper combined DTW with a voting algorithm — instead of just taking the nearest match, it runs DTW against multiple reference examples and uses weighted voting across all matches. This reduces false positives significantly.

Combined with MediaPipe keypoints from hand, body, AND face landmarks. Real-time performance.

Source: [Real-Time SL Recognition Using DTW and Voting (2025)](https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/S2773186325002166)

---

## Head-to-Head Comparison for Your Hackathon

| Approach | Accuracy (50 signs) | Training needed? | Setup time | Risk level | Impressiveness |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Raw DTW** (current plan) | ~75-85% | None | 1 hr | Low | Medium |
| **Angle-Based DTW** | ~85-92% | None | 1.5 hrs | Low | Medium-High |
| **Angle DTW + Voting** | ~88-94% | None | 2 hrs | Low | High |
| **LSTM on angle features** | ~90-95% | 30-60 min training | 3 hrs | Medium | High |
| **Prototypical Network** | ~85-90% (few-shot) | 2-4 hrs training | 4-5 hrs | High | **Very High** |
| **Soft DTW + encoder** | ~88-93% | 1-2 hrs training | 3-4 hrs | Medium-High | High |

---

## My Recommendation for the Hackathon

**Use Angle-Based DTW with Voting** as the primary engine. Here's why:

1. **No training needed** — zero risk of "the model didn't converge and we wasted 3 hours"
2. **Massive accuracy boost** over raw DTW — just from better features
3. **Same simplicity** — record reference signs, match against them
4. **Invariant to position/scale** — demo works even if the signer moves around
5. **Voting reduces false positives** — more reliable for judges
6. **Implementable in ~2 hours** — same effort as basic DTW

Then add a **small LSTM trained on your recorded reference data** as a secondary/verification model. If the LSTM and DTW agree, confidence is very high. If they disagree, show lower confidence. This ensemble approach is more robust than either alone.

**Updated accuracy estimate with Angle DTW + Voting:**
- Controlled demo conditions: **~93-97%**
- Same signer as references: **~88-94%**
- Different signer: **~75-85%**

That's a genuine step-change from the 75-85% I estimated for basic DTW.

Want me to update the build plan with this approach?

## Claude · 2026-04-11T21:35:39.173Z

This changes everything. I found a pretrained model on HuggingFace that's **ready to use right now** and blows DTW out of the water.

---

## The Game-Changer: TGCN-WLASL on HuggingFace

[`sharonn18/tgcn-wlasl`](https://huggingface.co/sharonn18/tgcn-wlasl) — a Temporal Graph Convolutional Network pretrained on WLASL, MIT licensed, ready for inference immediately.

| Feature | Detail |
|---|---|
| **Signs recognized** | **100, 300, 1,000, or 2,000** (pick your variant) |
| **Top-1 accuracy** | **>85%** |
| **Top-5 accuracy** | **>92%** |
| **Input** | MediaPipe pose keypoints (55 keypoints × 50 frames × 2 coords) |
| **Pretrained weights** | Yes — download and run, no training needed |
| **Framework** | PyTorch, runs on CPU |
| **License** | MIT |
| **Setup** | `pip install torch huggingface_hub`, ~10 lines to load |

**This means we don't need DTW as the primary engine at all.** We can load a pretrained model that recognizes 2,000 ASL signs at >85% accuracy in about 30 minutes of setup.

### Updated Pipeline

```
Webcam → MediaPipe extracts 55 pose keypoints per frame
    → Buffer 50 frames → shape (1, 55, 100)
    → Feed to pretrained TGCN model
    → Output: probability distribution over 2,000 signs
    → Top prediction + real confidence score
    → Claude assembles gloss → English sentence
    → TTS speaks it
```

### How This Stacks Against Everything Else

| Approach | Signs | Accuracy | Setup time | Training? |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Claude guessing (current) | Unlimited but random | ~15-25% | 0 | No |
| Raw DTW | 30-50 recorded | ~75-85% | 3 hrs | No (record refs) |
| Angle-based DTW | 30-50 recorded | ~85-92% | 3 hrs | No (record refs) |
| **TGCN pretrained** | **2,000** | **>85%** | **30 min** | **No** |
| Prototypical Network | Expandable | ~77% top-5 | 4-5 hrs | Yes |

The TGCN model is strictly better than DTW — more signs, higher accuracy, faster setup. It uses the same MediaPipe keypoints we're already extracting.

---

## Updated Architecture

**Primary engine:** TGCN pretrained model (2,000 signs, >85%)
**Secondary/supplement:** Angle-based DTW with voting (for custom signs not in WLASL, or as ensemble verification)
**Fingerspelling:** Rule-based classifier on hand landmarks
**Sentence assembly:** Claude Haiku converts ASL gloss → grammatical English

---

## Demo Sentences That Will Work

Since the TGCN is trained on WLASL's 2,000 most common ASL signs, here are demo conversations using signs that are definitely in the vocabulary:

### Demo 1: Greeting (warm, simple, judges see it work)

```
DEAF: signs HELLO
→ Echo Dot: "Hello"

HEARING: "Hi, nice to meet you"
→ Screen: "HI, NICE TO MEET YOU"

DEAF: signs THANK-YOU
→ Echo Dot: "Thank you"
```

### Demo 2: Daily need (practical, relatable)

```
DEAF: signs HELP PLEASE
→ Echo Dot: "Help, please"

HEARING: "What do you need?"
→ Screen: "WHAT DO YOU NEED?"

DEAF: signs WATER
→ Echo Dot: "Water"

HEARING: "Here you go"
→ Screen: "HERE YOU GO"
```

### Demo 3: Emergency / healthcare (gut-punch for judges)

```
DEAF: signs PAIN HELP
→ Echo Dot: "Pain. Help."

HEARING: "Where does it hurt?"
→ Screen: "WHERE DOES IT HURT?"

DEAF: signs HEAD
→ Echo Dot: "Head"

HEARING: "I'll get a doctor"
→ Screen: "I'LL GET A DOCTOR"
```

### Demo 4: Fingerspelling (shows technical depth)

```
DEAF: fingerspells D-A-C-T-Y-L
→ Screen builds: "D... DA... DAC... DACT... DACTY... DACTYL"
→ Echo Dot: "Dactyl"
```

### Demo 5: Emotional (makes judges feel something)

```
DEAF: signs HAPPY MEET YOU
→ Echo Dot: "Happy to meet you"

HEARING: "Me too, this is amazing"
→ Screen: "ME TOO, THIS IS AMAZING"
```

### Signs to Pre-Validate (all in WLASL 2000)

| Category | Signs to test and confirm work |
|---|---|
| Greetings | HELLO, GOODBYE, THANK-YOU, PLEASE, SORRY, YES, NO |
| Questions | WHAT, WHERE, WHO, WHEN, HOW, WHY |
| Needs | HELP, WATER, FOOD, BATHROOM, MEDICINE, PAIN |
| People | MOTHER, FATHER, FRIEND, DOCTOR, TEACHER |
| Emotions | HAPPY, SAD, GOOD, BAD, LOVE |
| Actions | WANT, NEED, GO, COME, WAIT, UNDERSTAND, KNOW |
| Adjectives | BIG, SMALL, HOT, COLD, MORE |
| Nouns | SCHOOL, HOUSE, PHONE, BOOK, NAME |

**Before the demo:** Test every sign you plan to use. Pick the 15-20 that work most reliably. Build your demo script around only those signs. If a sign is flaky, cut it.

---

## Prototypical Network — Can It Be Done Fast?

Based on the research:

**Existing implementations:**
- [`orobix/Prototypical-Networks-for-Few-shot-Learning-PyTorch`](https://github.com/orobix/Prototypical-Networks-for-Few-shot-Learning-PyTorch) — clean PyTorch implementation, well-documented
- A blog post claims "Your Own Few-Shot Model Ready in 15 Minutes" (though the page has since moved)
- Post-stroke gesture recognition using prototypical networks has a ready implementation at [`HSarwat/Few-Shot-Proto-TL`](https://github.com/HSarwat/Few-Shot-Proto-TL)

**Realistic timeline for prototypical network:**
- Download and adapt existing PyTorch implementation: ~1 hour
- Extract MediaPipe keypoints from recorded sign videos: ~30 min
- Train episodically on your recorded signs (50 signs × 3-5 examples): ~30-60 min on CPU
- Wire into the app: ~30 min
- **Total: ~2.5-3 hours** if nothing goes wrong

**The value of prototypical networks for the demo:**
- "Show it a sign 3 times and it learns it" — live on stage, add a new sign during the pitch
- This is the **wow moment** for judges: the system learns in real-time

**My recommendation:** Use TGCN as primary (instant, 2000 signs, >85%). If you have time left, add prototypical as a "learn new signs" feature. But don't risk the core demo on it.

---

| Priority | What | Engine | Time |
|---|---|---|---|
| **1 (must have)** | TGCN pretrained model integration | 2000 signs, >85% | 1-2 hrs |
| **2 (must have)** | Speech→text for hearing person | Google STT (already works) | 0 hrs |
| **3 (must have)** | Branding, UI, audience view | Dactyl | 2 hrs |
| **4 (should have)** | Fingerspelling classifier | Rule-based on landmarks | 1.5 hrs |
| **5 (should have)** | Angle-based DTW as backup/ensemble | For signs not in WLASL | 2 hrs |
| **6 (nice to have)** | Prototypical network "learn new signs" | Wow factor for judges | 2.5-3 hrs |
| **7 (must have)** | Test + rehearse demo | Pick 15-20 reliable signs | 1.5 hrs |
| **8 (must have)** | Pitch deck | Using research docs | 1.5 hrs |

## Claude · 2026-04-11T21:38:29.136Z

I need to correct myself. The real numbers are significantly different from what the HuggingFace page claims.

## The Honest Numbers

**What the HuggingFace page claims:** >85% Top-1

**What published benchmarks actually show for Pose-TGCN on WLASL:**

| Subset | Signs | Pose-TGCN Top-1 | Best Model Top-1 (any method) |
|---|---|---|---|
| WLASL100 | 100 | **55%** | 93.5% (Swin-Transformer ensemble) |
| WLASL300 | 300 | **38%** | ~73% (MSNN) |
| WLASL1000 | 1,000 | **34%** | ~63% (MSNN) |
| WLASL2000 | 2,000 | **23%** | ~47% (MSNN) |

The ">85%" claim on HuggingFace is either inflated, measured on a different test split, or refers to their enhanced version (they added Multi-Head Attention + Residual Connections, which aren't in the original Pose-TGCN). Without independent verification, I can't confirm it.

## What This Means

**Pose/keypoint-based models (what we can run on CPU) are fundamentally less accurate than video-based models (which need GPU).** The reason: keypoints throw away appearance information (hand texture, finger overlap, subtle shape differences) that video-based models can see.

| Approach | WLASL100 Top-1 | Needs GPU? |
|---|---|---|
| Pose-TGCN (keypoints only) | 55% | No |
| I3D (video-based) | 65% | Yes |
| VideoMAE (video-based) | 75% | Yes |
| Full Transformer (video-based) | 80% | Yes |
| MSNN multi-stream | 81% | Yes |
| Swin-Transformer ensemble (SOTA) | 93.5% | Yes (heavy) |

**The gap between pose-based (~55%) and video-based (~80-93%) is massive.** This is because ASL signs that look identical as keypoint skeletons (same finger positions) can look different in video (different hand orientation, contact point, subtle curl).

## Realistic Accuracy for Your Demo

Given all this, here's what to actually expect:

| Scenario | TGCN (if it works) | Angle-Based DTW | Combined |
|---|---|---|---|
| 100-sign vocab, same signer, controlled | ~55-70%* | ~85-92% | ~88-94% |
| 100-sign vocab, different signer | ~40-55% | ~70-80% | ~75-85% |
| 2000-sign vocab | ~23-35% | N/A (only recorded signs) | ~23-35% |
| Demo conditions (cherry-picked signs) | ~70-80% | ~90-95% | **~93-97%** |

*The HuggingFace model may genuinely be better than published Pose-TGCN due to architectural improvements — we won't know until we test it.

## The Honest Recommendation

**Angle-based DTW should be the primary engine, not the TGCN model.** Here's why:

1. DTW on your own recorded references will be more accurate than a pretrained model for your specific signer and vocabulary
2. You control the reference data — you know exactly which signs work
3. No "black box" risk — if DTW fails, you understand why
4. The TGCN model's claimed accuracy is unverified

**Use TGCN as a secondary signal:**
- Run both DTW and TGCN on the same input
- If they agree → high confidence (show green)
- If DTW matches but TGCN disagrees → medium confidence (show yellow)
- If neither matches → unknown (show gray)

This ensemble approach gives you the reliability of DTW with the breadth of TGCN as a backup.

## Updated Architecture

```
Primary:   Angle-Based DTW with Voting (~85-92% on your recorded signs)
Secondary: TGCN pretrained model (~55-70% on WLASL100, unverified higher)
Ensemble:  Both agree = high confidence, one matches = medium, neither = unknown
Fallback:  Fingerspelling classifier for individual letters
Assembly:  Claude Haiku converts ASL gloss → English
```

**For the demo: only show signs where both engines agree.** Test beforehand, find the 15-20 signs where both DTW and TGCN reliably produce the correct answer. Build your demo script around those. Under those conditions: **~93-97% accuracy.**

I should have dug into the real benchmarks before citing the HuggingFace claim. The corrected picture is: DTW is your reliable workhorse, TGCN adds breadth but isn't as accurate as advertised.

Yes — you both have access. Git handles version control perfectly for two people. Here's how to split it:

**Don't split by file halves — split by module.** Each person works on different files so you don't get merge conflicts.

### Suggested Split

**Naomi (you + your Claude):**
- `keypoint_extractor.py` (MediaPipe Holistic)
- `sign_buffer.py` (temporal frame buffer)
- `sign_matcher.py` (angle-based DTW engine)
- `fingerspell.py` (letter classifier)
- `reference_recorder.py` (recording tool)

**Tomisin (+ their Claude):**
- `app.py` rewrite (Flask integration of all modules)
- `language.py` (Claude sentence assembly)
- `speech.py` (TTS upgrade)
- All three templates: `dashboard.html`, `glasses.html`, `audience.html`

### Git Workflow
1. Both of you work on `main` branch (it's a hackathon, branches add overhead)
2. **Pull before you start:** `git pull origin main`
3. **Commit often:** after each file is done
4. **Pull before pushing:** `git pull origin main` then `git push`
5. Since you're working on different files, you won't get merge conflicts

**Or** — since I'm building for you and Tomisin likely has their own Claude — I build the entire backend (the ML pipeline, all the .py files) and Tomisin's Claude builds the frontend (templates + styling). That's the cleanest split.

## Claude · 2026-04-11T22:29:56.437Z

The remote only has what we pushed. If she runs `git pull` on her computer, she'll get the Dactyl v2 code. But if she has local changes she never committed/pushed, those would get overwritten or conflict.

**Tell her to:**
1. `git stash` (saves her local changes safely)
2. `git pull origin main` (gets Dactyl v2)
3. If she wants her old stuff back later: `git stash pop`

Or if her "version 3" has stuff worth keeping, tell her to commit and push it to a branch first:
```
git checkout -b tomisin-v3
git add -A && git commit -m "Tomisin v3"
git push origin tomisin-v3
```

Then switch to main and pull the new code:
```
git checkout main && git pull origin main
```

That way nothing is lost. Her v3 lives on a branch, Dactyl v2 is on main.

The app started and printed the Dactyl banner. Three issues to fix (all minor):

1. **Camera not authorized** — macOS needs you to grant camera permission to Terminal/VS Code
2. **PyAudio missing** — needs `brew install portaudio && pip install pyaudio`
3. **Port 5000 in use** — AirPlay Receiver on macOS uses 5000. Need to change to 5001 or disable AirPlay.

But first — your question about Anthropic credits:

**For students:** Anthropic doesn't have a formal student program, but:

1. **Free credits for hackathons** — Anthropic often sponsors hackathons with free API credits. Check if MorganHacks has an Anthropic sponsor/partner
2. **Free tier** — The Anthropic API gives you $5 free credits when you sign up at [console.anthropic.com](https://console.anthropic.com). Claude Haiku is cheap (~$0.25/million input tokens) so $5 goes a long way
3. **GitHub Student Developer Pack** — check if Anthropic is listed as a partner (some AI companies give student credits through this)
4. **Build with Claude program** — Anthropic sometimes runs programs for builders. Check their website/Discord

For this demo, Claude Haiku costs almost nothing — a full day of hackathon usage would be maybe $0.50-1.00. The $5 free signup credits are more than enough.

## Naomi · 2026-04-11T23:02:28.982Z

you used basically the same set up design as what was orginally in the repo?

## Claude · 2026-04-11T23:02:45.940Z

Yeah, mostly. The skeleton is the same — Flask + SocketIO + threaded camera loops + WebSocket events to the frontend. What changed is everything under the hood:

| Part | Original | Now |
|---|---|---|
| **Recognition** | Claude guesses from "fingers extended = index, middle" | DTW matching on 54 angle-based features over 30-60 frame sequences |
| **Features extracted** | 5 binary finger states + wrist x,y | 20 joint angles per hand + 6 face features + 8 pose features = 54 invariant features per frame |
| **Temporal** | Single frame, polled every 1.5s | Rolling buffer of 60 frames, emits when signing stops |
| **Face/body** | None | MediaPipe Holistic — face mesh + body pose |
| **Confidence** | Hardcoded "94%" | Real DTW distance, normalized, with margin penalty |
| **Fingerspelling** | None | Dedicated classifier with stability buffer |
| **Claude's role** | Guessing signs (wrong use) | Assembling ASL gloss → English sentences (right use) |
| **TTS** | pyttsx3 robot voice | Edge TTS neural voice with fallback |
| **UI** | "ASL Translator" | "Dactyl — Communication, Unbroken", mode toggle, signing indicator, audience view |

The Flask routes, SocketIO events, camera threading pattern, and HTML/JS structure are all evolved from what was there. Didn't reinvent what already worked.

## Claude · 2026-04-12T01:07:08.911Z

**What's running right now:**

| Component | What the plan said | What's actually running |
|---|---|---|
| **Keypoint extraction** | MediaPipe Holistic (hands + face + body, 543 keypoints) | MediaPipe Holistic — **yes, this is running** |
| **Sign recognition** | Angle-based DTW matching on temporal sequences | Built and running, but **0 reference signs recorded** — needs you to run `reference_recorder.py` |
| **Fingerspelling** | Rule-based ASL letter classifier | **Built and running** — toggle to fingerspell mode to test |
| **Speech-to-text** | Google Speech Recognition (hearing person → text) | **Running** — it already captured speech earlier |
| **TTS** | Edge TTS neural voice (sign → spoken aloud) | **Built** — will speak when a sign is matched |
| **Sentence assembly** | Claude Haiku (ASL gloss → English) | **Built** — will activate when signs are matched |

**The MediaPipe limitation you're thinking of:** MediaPipe 0.10.33 (latest) dropped the legacy `solutions` API. I downgraded to 0.10.14 which still has it. It's working fine — that's what's drawing the skeleton on the camera feed right now.

**Whisper:** I mentioned Whisper as an upgrade for speech-to-text (more accurate than Google Speech Recognition). It's **not running right now** — we're using Google STT which is what the original repo had. Whisper would need to run server-side on your Hetzner GPU or locally. For the hackathon, Google STT works — it's already picking up speech. Whisper is a Phase 1 upgrade.

**The one thing blocking the demo:** You need to record reference signs. The DTW engine is ready but has nothing to match against. That's the `python reference_recorder.py` step.

# Bidirectional ASL Translation Glasses — Deep Research Report

## Executive Summary

**True bidirectional ASL↔English translation glasses do not exist as a shipping consumer product today.** What exists is a fragmented landscape of:
- **ASL→English** (sign recognition): Research prototypes with promising accuracy in labs, no consumer glasses product
- **English→ASL** (sign generation): AI avatar systems reaching early commercial deployment, but on screens — not glasses
- **Caption glasses** (speech→text on lens): Multiple shipping products, but these are one-directional speech-to-text, not sign language translation

The closest thing to bidirectional is **Sorenson OmniBridge** (SDK, not glasses) and the **SignGlass** research prototype (ASL→English only, in glasses form factor). Nobody has combined both directions in a wearable.

---

## Part 1: Products & Companies

### A. Caption/Subtitle Glasses (Speech→Text — NOT sign language)

These are shipping products but they only convert speech to text captions on the lens. They help deaf/HoH users read what hearing people say, but don't translate sign language.

| Product | Price | Weight | Battery | Key Feature |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **XRAI AR2** | $750 (pre-order, RRP $880) | 49g | 8 hours | Full-lens display, 700% battery improvement over AR1, beam-forming mic, deaf-led design team |
| **Hearview** | Sold out (alternative: MYVU) | 52g | Not specified | 95% speech-to-text accuracy, 30+ languages, 10 speaker detection |
| **XanderGlasses** | Several hundred $ more than others | Not specified | Not specified | Fully standalone — no phone needed |
| **TranscribeGlass 2.0** | ~$95 | Not specified | Not specified | Stanford/Yale founders, cheapest option, Bluetooth to phone caption apps |
| **Leion Hey2** (LLVision) | Not disclosed | 49g | 6-8 hrs | CES 2026 debut, 100+ spoken languages, <500ms latency, 30K+ units shipped (gen 1), 10K+ pre-orders day one. No camera, no sign language support. |
| **Captify** | Not specified | Not specified | Concerns about all-day use | Noise cancellation, multiple languages |
| **SubLinq** | Not specified | Not specified | Not specified | Has "Deaf Mode", multi-language, teleprompting |

**Key insight**: These are ALL speech-to-text, not sign language translation. The deaf person reads captions but still can't sign back. It's half the equation.

Sources: [XRAI AR2](https://xrai.glass/ar2/), [Hearview](https://www.hearview.ai/), [XanderGlasses](https://www.xanderglasses.com/xanderglasses), [TranscribeGlass](https://mymodernmet.com/transcribeglass/), [Leion Hey2 at CES](https://www.tomorrowsworldtoday.com/technology/ces-2026-worlds-first-ai-powered-ar-translation-glasses-set-to-debut/), [Hearview 2025 Review](https://www.hearview.ai/blogs/news/which-smart-glasses-are-best-for-the-deaf-and-hard-of-hearing-a-2025-review-of-caption-glasses)

---

### B. Sign Language Recognition (ASL→English) — Glasses Form Factor

| Product/Project | Stage | What it does | Accuracy | Key limitation |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **SignGlass** (Penn State, UIST 2025) | Research prototype | 3 cameras on glasses capture hand AND facial markers, translates continuous ASL→English | WER 16.98%, BLEU-1 83%. 14 Deaf participants in user study, 39/46 said they'd use it daily | Research only — no product timeline |
| **SignGlasses** (commercial) | Shipping (remote interpreting) | Smart glasses with LIVE HUMAN interpreters (not AI). Interpreter views through glasses camera, signs/captions back. Two-way via human. | Human accuracy | It's human interpreting piped to glasses, NOT automated AI translation |
| **Raspberry Pi DIY glasses** (Nekhil, open source) | Maker project | Pi Zero 2 W + camera, recognizes ASL fingerspelling only (individual letters, not signs) | Limited — letters only | Can't do continuous signing, cloud-dependent, $15 hardware |
| **TransASL** (ACM IUI 2023) | Research | Smart glass ASL recognizer for daily life | Not specified | Older work, SignGlass supersedes it |

**Key insight**: SignGlass is the state of the art for ASL recognition in glasses. It's the first to capture non-manual markers (facial expressions) via dual cameras, which is crucial because those carry grammatical meaning in ASL. But it's a lab prototype, not a product.

Sources: [SignGlass paper (UIST 2025)](https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3746059.3747782), [SignGlasses](https://www.signglasses.com/), [Raspberry Pi glasses](https://www.raspberrypi.com/news/sign-language-translation-glasses/), [TransASL](https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3581641.3584071)

### C. Sign Language Generation (English→ASL) — Avatar Systems

These generate sign language from text/speech, displayed as 3D avatars on screens. None run on glasses yet.

| Company/Product | Stage | Approach | Key detail |
|---|---|---|---|
| **VSL Labs** (Israel) | Commercial API | Generative AI + NLP + Computer Vision. 3D avatars with facial expressions, emotion detection. Uses GPT-4 + in-house models. | Partnered with XRAI Glass (Sept 2024) for dual-mode: subtitles + sign language avatar on glasses. Top 100 Computer Vision company (F6S 2024). |
| **Signapse** (Cambridge, UK) | Commercial, shipping | Photo-realistic AI signers. Library of human sign videos + AI blending/stitching. Generates 5,000+ BSL train announcements daily. | Deployed at Network Rail, CVS, NHS England. ASL + BSL. £2M seed funding (June 2024). |
| **GenASL** (AWS) | Technical demo | Speech→text→ASL gloss→avatar video. Uses AWS Bedrock, Step Functions, Amplify. | Enterprise-focused, AWS infrastructure |
| **SignAvatar** | Commercial | PA system audio→text→sign language in 3-4 seconds. ASL + International Sign. | Transport/airport focus. Backed by Bosch, Microsoft, NVIDIA, Google for Startups. |
| **Hand Talk** (acquired by Sorenson) | Commercial | AI avatar translation. Pioneer in automated sign language via digital avatars. | Acquired Jan 2025 by Sorenson. |
| **Sign-Speak** (Deaf-led, US) | Pilot stage | Bidirectional: ASL→English AND English→ASL via photorealistic AI signer. | Piloted at Syracuse University (Nov 2025). Deaf-owned. Expanding to BSL, International Sign, Polish, Indian, Irish sign. |
| **Apple Research** (CHI 2025) | Research | 3-stage pipeline: GPT-4o text→gloss→skeletal pose→photorealistic video. Non-manual markers via expression blending. | 30 DHH participant study. BLEU-4: 0.276 for text→gloss (10x better than prior neural approaches). Eyebrow generation for questions. Severe limitation: conditional detection only 5% recall. |

**Key insight**: The XRAI + VSL Labs partnership is the closest to sign language on glasses — combining XRAI's caption glasses with VSL's sign language avatars. But this is announced, not shipping. Sign-Speak is the most promising for true bidirectional because they're Deaf-led and handle both directions.

Sources: [VSL Labs](https://www.vsllabs.com/technology), [Signapse](https://www.signapse.ai/), [GenASL (AWS)](https://aws.amazon.com/blogs/machine-learning/genasl-generative-ai-powered-american-sign-language-avatars/), [SignAvatar](https://www.signavatar.org/), [XRAI + VSL partnership](https://xrai.glass/blog/xrai-partners-with-vsl-labs/), [Sign-Speak at Syracuse](https://news.syr.edu/2025/11/12/university-piloting-ai-powered-asl-interpreting-service-sign-speak/), [Apple NMM research](https://machinelearning.apple.com/research/ai-sign-language-generation)

### D. Bidirectional Translation Systems (Both Directions)

| System | Form factor | Directions | Stage | How it works |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Sorenson OmniBridge** | SDK (runs on AI PC, no internet needed) | ASL→English text + English speech→text for deaf user to read | Commercial SDK | Camera captures signing → AI translates → hearing person reads. Hearing person speaks → captioned for deaf user. First scalable SDK. Acquired by Sorenson (Jan 2025) from Intel. |
| **Sign-Speak** | Web/app/QR code | ASL→English + English→ASL (photorealistic AI signer) | Pilot (Syracuse Univ) | Deaf-owned. Full bidirectional with AI avatar. |
| **Research prototype** (MDPI 2025) | Web interface | ASL↔English using CNN + Transformer | Academic paper | Lab system, not a product |

**No glasses-form-factor bidirectional product exists.** OmniBridge is closest to a real product but runs on a PC, not glasses.

Sources: [Sorenson OmniBridge acquisition](https://sorenson.com/newsroom/enterprise/sorenson-acquires-omnibridge-and-hand-talk-to-develop-automated-sign-language-translation-capabilities/), [OmniBridge](https://omnibridge.ai/), [Sign-Speak](https://www.closingthegap.com/sign-speak-real-time-asl-translation/), [Bidirectional research (MDPI)](https://www.mdpi.com/2073-431X/15/1/20)

### E. Big Tech Efforts

| Company | What they're doing | Stage |
|---|---|---|
| **Google** | **SignGemma** — on-device ASL→English model built on Gemini Nano. Vision transformer for hands/face/motion + compact language model. Trained on 10,000+ hours annotated ASL video. <200ms translation on-device. No internet needed. | Developer preview available, broader release planned Q4 2025 |
| **Apple** | CHI 2025 research on text→ASL video generation with non-manual markers (detailed above) | Research paper |

| **Microsoft** | Backed SignAvatar. No direct sign language glasses product. | Investment/partnership |

**Google SignGemma is the biggest deal** — first major on-device ASL recognition model from a big tech company, and it's designed for phones/tablets/laptops (not glasses specifically, but the on-device nature means it could theoretically run on future AR glasses).

Sources: [Google SignGemma](https://multilingual.com/google-signgemma-on-device-asl-translation/), [Apple NMM (arxiv)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2502.05661), [Meta edge AI](https://www.zenml.io/llmops-database/edge-ai-architecture-for-wearable-smart-glasses-with-real-time-multimodal-processing)
